## Phase 15 — AI Visual Director

### Objective
Decide, per scene, the educational visual strategy: what is being taught, what the learner should understand, what
should carry the explanation (a step flow, a timeline, the formula symbol by symbol, code beside its output, both sides
of a comparison, a diagram, key points…), what the presenter does, what is emphasised and what the camera and motion
should mean. The result is a structured visual direction plan that the existing systems consume: the Visual Router
(Phase 4) as a preference, the Presenter Director (Phase 12) as an interaction, the Intelligent Scene Composer
(Phase 14) as composition preferences, and so the Phase 13 page (preview = playback = export). The director never
generates media and never becomes a second router, composer or renderer. Phase 16 (presenter/visual synchronization)
was not started.

### Baseline (start of Phase 15)
Backend 373/373, frontend 140/140, Phase 1 27/27, Phase 2 30/30, export with library assets 35/35, Phase 3 26/26,
Phase 4 24/24, Phase 5 11/11, Phase 6 17/17, Phase 7 18/18, Phase 8 12/12, Phase 9 14/14, Phase 10 17/17,
Phase 11 18/18, Phase 12 20/20, Phase 13 24/24, Phase 14 17/17.

### Audit (before Phase 15, from the code; two read-only analysts plus the orchestrator)
- The Visual Router runs only on the server (`visuals.py` `plan_lesson`, five callers: the page's plan, Visual Review,
  stored runs, the lesson batch, recovery's `still_wanted`). Its only per-scene preference input was the screenplay's
  `visual` object, which also feeds generation prompts and fingerprints, so a director must not write it.
- Visuals are planned before composition; the composer reads the router's `visual_plan` (Phase 14 `scene_intent`).
- The screenplay already carries structure: `concept_map` with `concept_id` per scene (used only by the skill tree and
  export chapters), `definition` / `keyword` / callout markup, `pre code`, tables, `takeaway-list`, `[SYNC]` markers.
  No scene carries source references or a learner level; no timeline/date detection existed anywhere.
- Phase 14's `scene_intent` already classifies purpose, content and density from the board; Phase 11 has formula,
  definition and code detectors; Phase 12 has presenter gestures/expressions/behaviours per profile.

### Architecture
```
 screenplay scene + lesson (concept map, earlier scenes)
   ─► scene understanding (visual_director.understand): concept, terms, definition term, formula symbols and their
      meanings, code and its output, steps, dated events, the two sides of a comparison, the visual the screenplay asks
      for and the one the router really has, presenter, learner level, duration (Phase 14's board parser and purpose
      rules, Phase 11's detectors; no model)
   ─► 1–3 rule candidates (+1 from a text model for an ambiguous scene in AI-assisted mode)
   ─► hard constraints, repair, deterministic score, fallback; the user's choices on top
   ─► scene.visual_direction (data only)
        ├─ route ─────────────► Visual Router (preference: prefer existing, preferred media, match terms)
        ├─ presenter.interaction ► Presenter Director (gesture/expression within the profile)
        └─ the rest ──────────► Intelligent Scene Composer (presenter role, visual roles, representation → board style,
                                 camera/motion intent → Phase 13 moves, annotations → labels, emphasis, reveal)
                                 ─► Phase 13 page (one renderer: preview, playback, export) · Visual Review
```
Responsibilities stay where they were: Phase 15 says what should teach; Phase 14 composes; Phase 4 obtains the visual;
Phase 12 presents; Phase 13 renders; Phase 6 review stays authoritative. Classic lessons are untouched (the director
runs in the cinematic style only).

### Scene understanding (`visual_director.understand`)
- **Reused, not re-parsed:** Phase 14's board parser (`scene_intent._Board`, now also keeping list-item texts, table
  headers, keyword spans, paragraphs, the definition's text and the block after a code block) and its purpose rules
  (`scene_intent.classify`, extracted so both layers share one authority); Phase 11's formula detector
  (`formula_parts`); the lesson's `concept_map` and each scene's `concept_id`.
- **Signals:** the concept and its key terms, the defined term, a formula's symbols and what they stand for in the
  scene's own words ("F = Force", or a word equation "Force = Mass × Acceleration" lined up with F = ma), code and
  whether the scene says what it produces, ordered steps, dated events (new: years, centuries, BCE/AD), the two sides
  of a comparison (table headers, "A vs B" titles, two lists), worked examples, mechanism / analogy / cycle /
  algorithm wording, numbers, the presenter (Phase 12's per-scene plan or the lesson placement), the learner level
  (a new optional setting), the narration's length and `[SYNC]` reveals.
- **Two visuals, kept apart:** what the screenplay asks for (`asked_visual`, the only input of the router's
  preference) and what the scene will really show (the router's plan when there is one: media-aware; a router plan
  that found nothing means no visual; a removed visual means none).

### Strategy vocabulary (bounded, one definition)
12 families, 30 strategies: conceptual (concept overview, definition with a simple picture, analogy, diagram-first),
structural (parts of the whole, relationship map), process (step by step, workflow, life cycle, algorithm), temporal
(timeline), comparative (side by side, before/after), mathematical (formula symbol by symbol, equation built up, graph,
numerical example), programming (code walkthrough, code and its output), scientific (how it works, system diagram,
simulation), data (chart, table), demonstration (worked example, demonstration), assessment (question focus), summary
(key points, concept map, recap). What teaches (20 kinds: the board, a definition card, a step flow, a timeline, the
formula, code, code beside its output, a comparison, a table, key points, the question, a diagram, illustration, chart,
picture, animation, video, simulation, the presenter, nothing extra). Presenter roles (dominant, secondary, guide,
demonstrator, hidden) and interactions (introduces, explains, points to the visual / the board, pauses for the visual,
summarizes, asks). Semantic camera intents (static, slow zoom, focus, pan, follow the process, compare, wide to
detail, detail to wide) and motion intents (none, fade, reveal, progressive build, highlight, move along a path,
transform, compare, sequence, zoom to detail). Reason codes with fixed short sentences (never model reasoning).

### Rules, candidates and scoring
- **Rules per scene kind** (first choice first; 1–3 candidates): introduction → the presenter introduces the concept;
  definition → the definition on its own card (its diagram supporting), or diagram-first; process → a step flow built
  step by step, the presenter as a small guide pointing at the board; timeline → the events on a timeline in order;
  formula → the formula, symbol by symbol, its symbols labelled, the camera leaning in (several formulas: built up);
  code → code beside its output when the scene says what it produces, else a walkthrough, the presenter away and a still
  camera; comparison → both sides together, a still camera; diagram (a visual with little text) → diagram-first, the
  presenter small and pointing (Rule G: small when the visual evidence is primary); example → a worked example;
  quiz → the question with minimal distraction; summary → key points as cards; simulation/video → the visual takes
  the stage, the presenter pauses.
- **Score** (deterministic, weights in code): learning-goal fit of the strategy family for the scene's purpose,
  representation (the content really supports it), clarity (one primary focus), density, what exists (a visual the
  scene has; an approved or explicit asset counts more), presenter compatibility, duration, continuity, simplicity
  for beginners. Ties go to the rules' order; an AI suggestion first and a small bonus.
- **Learning goal and focus** in plain words ("follow the 4 steps in order", "tell Plants and Animals apart",
  "read the formula and know what each symbol means"); a scene title that is not a concept name ("Inside a plant",
  "Quick check", "Worked example: …") is never used as one.

### Hard constraints, repair and fallback
- **Hard:** a representation needs its content (a step flow ≥ 2 steps, a timeline ≥ 3 dated events, code with output
  needs the output sentence, a formula, two sides, a definition, a question; a picture strategy needs a visual the scene
  really has); vocabulary only.
- **Repairs:** no presenter → hidden; a supporting visual that does not exist is dropped; a build needs things to
  build and time to build them (else everything together); code, quizzes and comparisons keep a still camera; dense
  text → no dominant presenter and a still camera; a demonstrator without a visual becomes a guide.
- **Fallback:** the board with the presenter beside it, revealed together, a still camera (or the question focus).
  The composer's own repairs and fallback still apply after; a lesson never fails because of the director.

### Cross-scene continuity
A bounded lesson memory (at most 40 concepts): concept (the concept map's id, else the title without "(Contd.)") →
the scene that first showed it, its representation and strategy, its terms. A later scene of the same concept prefers
the same representation where its content allows (a continuity score), records `continuity.from_scene` and says so;
it never overrides a representation the content calls for (a formula stays a formula).

### The user's choices (Visual Review)
`scene.visual_review.direction = {status: "changed", overrides}`: strategy, what teaches, presenter role, camera,
motion, preference (prefer existing / static). Applied on top of the director's own decision (source "user", the keys
locked); a choice the scene cannot support is set aside and said so ("your what teaches choice could not be kept: …");
a presenter role in a scene without a presenter is said so; "Automatic" gives one choice back; values outside the
vocabulary are ignored. A changed direction changes the composition's fingerprint, so an approved composition re-opens.

### AI-assisted direction (optional)
"Visual direction: Automatic / AI-assisted" (Automatic by default: no model call). Only undecided scenes are asked
(two valid candidates within 0.05; at most 6 per lesson, in parallel, within 25 s: a late answer is not waited for, a
scene past the sixth is recorded as not asked). The brief is structured and bounded (purpose, concept, learner level,
content types, density, duration, counts, sides, up to 5 key terms, formula symbols, the visuals the scene can show,
the rules' suggested strategies), declared as data. The answer is one JSON object of enum values (strategy, what
teaches — only from the available visuals —, presenter role, camera, motion, complexity, up to 3 emphasis targets, up
to 4 reason codes); one bounded repair; otherwise the rules decide. The validated suggestion is one more candidate,
checked by the same hard constraints. It is kept with the scene (keyed by what the screenplay says, the provider, the
model and the version, so it survives the router's and the Presenter Director's later steps) and rebuilt from the
server's own checks whenever it is reused (a record the page sends back is never trusted as sent). Phase 11's
`call_model` (Gemini / OpenAI, the composer's provider setting); the director's stand-in model on test servers.

### Integration (no responsibility moved)
- **Visual Router (Phase 4):** `scene.visual_direction.route` = `{prefer_existing: true, preferred_media, match_terms}`,
  derived only from what the screenplay asks for and the user's preference (stable across re-plans). The router reads it
  as `VisualRequest.preference` (`visuals.route_preference`, strictly validated, version 1): the slot's wanted media
  for library ranking (never a screenplay-fixed slot, never an AI video on a side slot), library before generation (it
  never turns AI on), match terms as a tie-breaking library bonus (never in a prompt, the AI cache identity or
  `still_wanted`). Rule order unchanged; no provider named. The preference is not part of a visual's review fingerprint.
- **Presenter Director (Phase 12):** `presenters.direction_interaction` maps the interaction onto the profile's existing
  gestures and expressions (introduces → welcome, points → point, summarizes → open hand, asks → listening, pauses →
  listening) only in the Director's own choice (the lesson's and Visual Review's choices win; nothing a profile cannot
  do; no timing, no sync — Phase 16).
- **Intelligent Scene Composer (Phase 14):** the direction is a soft layer over every candidate, below the screenplay's
  and the user's composition choices: presenter role (dominant/secondary/small/hidden; a guide stays beside the content
  for Aadhi, who has no small form), visual roles, the layouts that show the representation (a small score bonus),
  camera intent → a Phase 13 move, the board's representation, the reveal, emphasis targets, labels from the
  direction's annotations (only labels that add meaning: a formula's symbols; never a repeat of the board).
- **Phase 13 (one renderer):** data-driven board representations (`BOARD_STYLES`: a step flow with numbered cards and
  connectors, a timeline with date badges, code with an output card, key points as cards; styles only), a "together"
  reveal (a comparison), label chips read from the scene's direction (the plan points to them, never copies lesson
  text). The composition's fingerprint includes the direction's fingerprint. A router plan that found nothing gets no
  room in the layout (Phases 13 and 14 too; the page shows "No visual" in Visual Review).

### Visual Review and the page
- The composition inspector shows "Visual direction: Step-by-step process ✓", how it was decided, the goal, what
  teaches, the presenter and motion in plain words, the focus, up to 3 reasons, notes (e.g. "a diagram would help this
  scene; none is planned") and the AI outcome; "Change direction" (with "Automatic" for the user's own choices),
  "Back to automatic direction", "Regenerate direction" (never media). The composition's own controls are unchanged.
- Settings: "Visual direction: Automatic / AI-assisted" and "Learners: General / Beginner / Intermediate / Advanced".
- The planning chain (cinematic style only): the direction first (`POST /api/cinematic/direction`, saved with the
  lesson), then the router, the presenter and the composition (which returns the directions it followed). Review
  actions are composed in the page's lesson context.

### API
| Endpoint | |
|---|---|
| `POST /api/cinematic/direction` | every scene's direction (cinematic style; nothing generated); saved with the lesson |
| `POST /api/cinematic/direction/regenerate` | one scene's direction decided again (AI-assisted: a fresh suggestion), then its composition; never media |
| `POST /api/cinematic/direction/review` | the user's direction choices (change / reset; "auto" gives one back) |
| `POST /api/cinematic/plan`, `/review`, `/regenerate` | also return the direction(s) each composition followed; `/review` takes the page's scenes as context |
| `GET /api/cinematic` | `director`: modes, availability per provider, the vocabulary |

No database change: the direction lives in the scene (`visual_direction`, `visual_review.direction`).

### Files changed
- **New:** `visual_director.py`, `tests/test_visual_director.py`, `tests/direction.test.js`, `tests/direction_browser_check.mjs`.
- **Changed:** `scene_intent.py` (texts kept by the board parser, `classify` extracted, a found-nothing router plan is no
  visual), `composer.py` (the direction layer, board-style text estimates), `cinematic.py` (board representations,
  direction labels, reveal, the direction in the plan and the fingerprint, `found_nothing`, the direction endpoints and
  settings, review in the page's context), `visuals.py` (the router preference), `presenters.py` (the interaction),
  `cinematic.js` (renderer: representations, date badges, direction labels; the API client, the direction summary, the
  settings), `review.js` (the direction block and actions), `index.html` (the planning chain, the review adapter, the
  representation styles, the together reveal), `tests/test_visuals.py`, `tests/test_presenters.py`,
  `tests/presenter.test.js`.

### Results
Silent regression on the final code (Chrome with `--disable-audio-output`, speech stubbed). The full run was interrupted
once by the machine sleeping and slowed once by heavy load; every check affected was re-run on its own on the final
code (see Issues), and the unit suites were re-run last:

| Suite | Result |
|---|---|
| Backend (unittest) | **439/439** (373 before + 66 Phase 15) |
| Frontend (node --test) | **148/148** (140 before + 8 Phase 15) |
| Phase 1 mascot | 27/27 |
| Phase 2 export | 30/30 |
| Export with library assets | 35/35 |
| Phase 3 assets | 26/26 |
| Phase 4 visual router | 24/24 (re-run alone) |
| Phase 5 AI cache | 11/11 |
| Phase 6 Visual Review | 17/17 |
| Phase 7 recording performance (720p) | 18/18 (28.3 fps effective, 0 stalls, page 124.3 fps) |
| Phase 8 providers | 12/12 |
| Phase 9 recovery | 14/14 |
| Phase 10 Manim sandbox | 17/17 |
| Phase 11 source documents | 18/18 |
| Phase 12 presenter | 20/20 (re-run alone) |
| Phase 13 cinematic | 24/24 (re-run alone) |
| Phase 14 composer | 17/17 (final code; preview/export frame difference 5.6) |
| **Phase 15 browser check** | **23/23** (final code; preview/export frame difference 6.1) |
| Static checks | inline scripts and all JS files parse; browser checks parse; Python modules compile |

### LLM validation
**Not done for real.** No text model key exists on this machine. AI-assisted direction was verified only with the
director's stand-in model (valid, malformed-then-repaired, malformed, fabricated and failing answers, a slow model
against the budget) and with a real provider name and no key (unavailable: the rules decide). No real request was
made.

### Visual quality validation
A representative lesson (introduction, definition, process, diagram, the diagram's concept returning, formula, code,
comparison, timeline, quiz, summary; the lesson's own library diagrams; the Aadhi Teacher) was directed automatically,
rendered in real Chrome at 1280×720 and 1920×1080, and every screenshot inspected over two rounds (the first was not
accepted; see Issues):
- **Definition:** the definition on its own card with the term highlighted, the leaf diagram supporting it, the teacher
  beside both. Goal: "know what Photosynthesis means".
- **Process:** the four steps as numbered cards with connectors, built one by one with the narration (the current step
  highlighted), the teacher small and pointing. Goal: "follow the 4 steps of Photosynthesis in order".
- **Diagram:** diagram-first (the library diagram in the largest area, without labels that would repeat the board), the
  teacher small and pointing; the returning "(Contd.)" scene keeps the same representation (continuity recorded).
- **Formula:** F = ma on its card, the symbols labelled from the lesson's own words (F = Force · m = Mass ·
  a = Acceleration), the camera leaning in at the reveal.
- **Code:** the code highlighted with an "Output" card under it carrying the screenplay's sentence, no presenter, a still
  camera.
- **Comparison:** the table whole at once (both sides together), the teacher beside it, a still camera.
- **Timeline:** the events on a line with date badges (1837, 1946, 1971, 2007), revealed in order.
- **Quiz:** the question leads, the answers and the countdown; the teacher beside it.
- **Summary:** the key points as check-marked cards in two columns, revealed with the narration.
- **Introduction:** the hero title and the teacher welcoming with open arms (the presenter's "introduces" intent through
  Phase 12's existing gesture).

Measured in every scene at both sizes: nothing overlaps; no text shrinks below 80 % (the page's fit reported 0.84–1.0).
**Preview versus export:** the recording used the same directions (step flow, code with output, timeline, key points
seen during the recording) and its frame of the process scene matches the preview (mean difference 6.1 of 255 in the final run, 5.1 in an earlier one, on a
32×18 grid); the exported frame carries no player controls. No AI generation ran (the leaf diagram is the library asset,
reused).

### Issues found and fixed during the phase
- **Found by integrating with the existing tests (none changed for these):**
  - Phase 13's rule that a plan points to the lesson's text and never copies it: the first direction summary carried the
    learning goal and the label texts. The plan now carries codes and pointers; the texts live in the scene's direction.
  - A direction's "no motion" (for a simulation that moves by itself) switched off the lesson's slide transitions; only
    the user's "still" choice switches motion off now.
  - Diagram scenes made the presenter a demonstrator (secondary); Rule G and Phase 14 both want the presenter small
    when the visual evidence is primary.
  - The director, seeing only what the screenplay asks for, missed a visual the router placed; its strategy is now
    media-aware (the router preference stays screenplay-only, so routing and direction never feed each other).
- **Found by the backend test agent (each pinned by a test first):** a presenter switched off for one scene was still
  directed to lead; a router plan that found nothing still counted as a visual; "Worked example: …" was used as a
  concept name.
- **Found by looking at the renders:** term labels under a diagram repeated the board's list (and one came from the
  asset's search keywords): only labels that add meaning are made now; the timeline's dots were clipped at 1080p (the
  page's bullet rule enlarged them); the step flow's connector hung below the last visible step; the code-output card set
  the screenplay's sentence in a console font as if it were the program's output, and its side-by-side layout never
  applied (a container cannot query itself): it is always under the code now.
- **Found by the end-of-phase security audit and correctness review (two read-only agents; each verified, fixed and
  covered by a new test):**
  - A crafted scene (thousands of keywords, a title with a huge run of spaces) could stall the server for seconds:
    texts are bounded before any pattern runs; the direction review has the 200-scene cap.
  - A kept AI suggestion sent back by the page was trusted as sent (any provider/error text, extra decision keys,
    "AI" without a call; malformed ones gave a 500): it is rebuilt from the server's own checks.
  - In AI-assisted mode a stored direction was reused as sent: the server now always rebuilds directions and reuses
    only a re-validated suggestion.
  - A choice value outside the vocabulary (e.g. presenter role "small") gave a 500: ignored now.
  - Code beside its output was estimated as if side by side and fell back to the safe layout for ordinary code lines.
  - An AI suggestion made before routing was discarded after it (its key included the router's result): it is keyed by
    what the screenplay says and re-validated against what the scene can show.
  - Composition Keep and direction review composed in the saved lesson (without the other scenes' visual and presenter
    plans), so an approval could be stored for a different plan and label chips could mismatch: they compose in the
    page's lesson and return the direction they followed.
  - The user's motion choice "Still" kept the camera moving.
  - Malformed screenplay fields (keywords as a string or a number, a list as concept id) split terms per letter or gave
    a 500.
  - The router preference changed visual fingerprints whenever a lesson switched between the Classic and cinematic
    styles (re-opening approvals): it is no longer part of a visual's fingerprint (it only orders library matches).
- **Found by the final regression and an acceptance audit (a read-only agent checking the brief's two checklists):**
  - A strategy that contradicts the scene's content (a "timeline" for four undated steps, from the model or as a user's
    choice) was accepted: each strategy now states the content it needs (dates, steps, code, its output, a formula, two
    sides, a quiz, a moving visual), checked as a hard constraint; a user's such choice is set aside and said so.
  - Formula scenes made the presenter secondary; Rule G and Phase 14's own check want the presenter small beside the
    formula (the evidence): formula strategies now use the guide role.
  - With no model available, the honest "AI direction is not available" note disappeared once the composition rebuilt
    the directions (its record had no key to be recognised by): unavailable, skipped and late records are keyed now.
  - The direction block in Visual Review reused the composition's facts class, so "the inspector's facts" (Phase 13's
    check) read the direction's list: it has its own class.
  - The machine went to sleep during one run (`net::ERR_NETWORK_IO_SUSPENDED`, recordings cut off) and was heavily
    loaded in another (page loads over 30 s, a scene measured after it had moved on): each affected check was re-run on
    its own and passed; nothing in the code was changed for these.
- **Found earlier phases' gap:** a router plan that found nothing still got a visual area in Phases 13 and 14 (the page
  showed the concept map or a "no image" notice there); the content gets the room now.
- **Test expectations changed with the behaviour** (none weakened): the director test that expected a diagram term
  label (now: no labels that repeat the board); the router tests that expected a directed fingerprint to differ (now: the
  same, by design, and the preference still reaches the rules); one director understanding test that relied on an
  invalid settings combination (now: a presenter left out by Phase 12).

### Known limitations
- **The director chooses among what the lesson has.** It can say a diagram would help a scene ("visual_need": wanted)
  but never creates a visual request, a prompt or a slot; adding a visual stays a Visual Review action (the router and
  the AI media layer then decide how).
- **Representations are drawn from the lesson's own markup** (a list becomes a step flow or a timeline, a code block and
  the sentence after it become code with output); there is no new diagram generator, and the output card shows the
  screenplay's sentence, not the program's real output (nothing is executed).
- **Emphasis targets are scene-level** (the formula, the visual, the board, the presenter, a label); highlighting a
  single code line or a diagram node needs structured diagrams and timing that belong to Phase 16.
- **Continuity covers representations and terms** (and, through the router's match terms, the same library visual for
  the same concept); shared colours across scenes belong to Phase 17's styling system.
- **Source references:** scenes carry no link back to Phase 11's source sections, so the direction records the concept,
  not page or paragraph references.
- **AI-assisted direction** was verified with the stand-in model only (no text model key on this machine).

### Deferred to later phases
Phase 16 (presenter + visual synchronization: speech-to-gesture timing, word-level sync), Phase 17 (styling system,
lesson-wide colours), Phase 18 (quality engine), Phase 19 (editor), Phase 20, Phase 21. The understanding, the
strategy vocabulary and the direction plan are the extension points.

### Final status
**Complete.**
- **Verified end to end:** every scene of a representative lesson directed by purpose (definition, process, diagram,
  continuity, formula, code with output, comparison, timeline, quiz, summary, introduction), the direction reflected in
  the composition and the render at both sizes (nothing overlapping, text never below 80 %), the router's preference
  (library reuse, no generation), the presenter's interaction, Visual Review (the direction shown, the user's choices,
  "Automatic", Regenerate direction without media, approvals re-opening when the direction changes), a refresh keeping
  the directions, and the export recording the same directions as the preview.
- **Not verified:** AI-assisted direction with a real Gemini or OpenAI model (no key here; the stand-in model and the
  unavailable path were tested).
- **Unchanged:** the Classic style (the director runs in the cinematic style only); Automatic direction makes no model
  call; no Phase 16 synchronization.
- **Nothing committed or deployed.** No database change (the direction lives in the scene).

---

## Phase 16 — Presenter + Visual Synchronization

### Objective
Decide WHEN each element acts so that the narration, the presenter, the visual, the text, the labels and the camera
teach the same thing at the same moment: the diagram highlighted when the narration points at it, each step lit as it
is named, a formula's symbols labelled as their meanings are spoken, the code's output revealed when the narration says
what it prints, a comparison's side focused when it is named, the presenter pointing then explaining, the camera leaning
in at the key moment, the result kept visible. One structured synchronization plan per scene, played by the existing
Phase 13 page (preview = playback = export). Phase 17 (styling system) was not started.

### Baseline (start of Phase 16)
Backend 439/439, frontend 148/148, Phase 1 27/27, Phase 2 30/30, export with library assets 35/35, Phase 3 26/26,
Phase 4 24/24, Phase 5 11/11, Phase 6 17/17, Phase 7 18/18, Phase 8 12/12, Phase 9 14/14, Phase 10 17/17,
Phase 11 18/18, Phase 12 20/20, Phase 13 24/24, Phase 14 17/17, Phase 15 23/23.

### Audit (before Phase 16; two read-only analysts plus the orchestrator)
- **Narration:** split at `[PAUSE]` into segments, each played from its own server audio file (or browser speech in the
  default preview); `[SYNC]` reveals placed by character ratio of the segment's audio (`charIndex / cleanText.length ×
  audio.duration`), checked on `timeupdate` (~250 ms jitter); one sentence of subtitle at a time. No word or sentence
  boundaries anywhere (Edge-TTS's are discarded); Phase 12's speech timeline measures each segment's real duration
  (ffprobe) before playback.
- **Renderer:** the Phase 13 stage plays entrances, label items and highlights (some anchored to `[SYNC]` beats), one
  camera move per scene; no exits, no item-level highlights, the camera's start in absolute seconds; the stage clock
  started up to 0.8 s after the narration; an early beat during formula typesetting was lost (the beat count reset).
- **Presenter:** per-scene acting only (the drawn teacher's SVG built once per scene; Aadhi's narration states);
  capabilities per profile (Aadhi: no gestures; the teacher: 8 gestures, 8 expressions; an AI clip cannot act).
- **Preview vs export:** the same page and stage, but the default preview spoke with the browser's voice while the
  export recorded the server's audio (different durations and reveals).

### Architecture
```
 scene narration ─► segments (split at [PAUSE], as the page does) · [SYNC] beats · sentences · the words naming each concept
 visual direction (Phase 15: what to emphasise, the presenter's interaction, camera intent)
 composed scene (Phase 14/13 plan: the visual, the board's representation, labels, the presenter, the camera move)
 presenter capabilities (Phase 12: what it can change at a moment)
   ─► Synchronization Director (sync_director.py, deterministic; optional AI alignment for what the rules cannot place)
   ─► scene.cinematic_plan.sync — ONE plan per scene: semantic events anchored to the narration, compiled to narration
      POSITIONS {segment, ratio}, with estimated seconds as the fallback; attention; end hold; metrics; fingerprint
   ─► the Phase 13 page (cinematic.js CinematicStage) — the only runtime: preview = playback = export
        clock: the narration audio actually playing (requestAnimationFrame, media time) · browser speech beats · estimate
        dispatch: presenter acts (Phase 12) · visual / formula pulse · board item glow (fixed kinds) · output reveal ·
                  label chips in · camera move from the current framing
```
A position is the page's own rule for `[SYNC]` reveals (character index ÷ the segment's clean-text length): it does not
change when the voice, the audio file or the speech rate changes, so the timing never needs recompiling for those; new
narration text recompiles it (nothing else is regenerated). Responsibilities stay where they were: Phase 15 says what,
Phase 14 composes, Phase 13 renders, Phase 12 presents, the router obtains visuals, Visual Review approves.

### Narration and timing sources (reused, never a second TTS or subtitle engine)
- The page's segment split, `[SYNC]` character ratios and server audio per segment (Phase 12's audio path); Phase 12's
  measured segment durations are not needed at runtime because positions are ratios of the segment actually playing.
- No word or sentence boundaries exist anywhere (Edge-TTS's are discarded): sentence and concept anchors are placed by
  character ratio inside their segment — exact with the test server's speech tone (as long as its text), approximate
  with a real voice (speech is not uniform). Each moment carries a lateness budget (0.25 s, 0.35 s presenter, 0.4 s
  camera) that the browser check measures it against; the page never fires a moment more than 0.06 s early.
- Fallback clocks: browser speech (each segment's start is known; the estimate runs inside it); no narration or muted
  (the estimated seconds from the scene start).
- **Preview = export:** a synchronized scene now plays the SERVER audio in preview too (as scenes with a drawn or AI
  presenter already did), so the preview hears the voice the export records and follows the same clock; if the server
  voice fails for a segment in the preview, the browser's voice speaks that segment and its moments follow the speech
  clock from that segment on (never a silent scene); the next segment's server audio takes the clock back.

### Events (bounded vocabulary, only what the renderer and the presenter can really do)
| Type | When the director places it | What the page does |
|---|---|---|
| `diagram_focus` / `visual_highlight` | the sentence that points at the visual ("look at…"); a diagram's term when it is named (at most two `diagram_focus` moments per scene, the look cue included) | the visual pulses |
| `formula_emphasis` | the scene's first `[SYNC]` beat (the formula's reveal), else the sentence that states it | the formula pulses |
| `label_enter` | a symbol's label when its meaning is spoken ("Force"), a screenplay label at its `[SYNC]` | the chip enters with the labels' own entrance (`label_exit` is in the vocabulary and the page can play it; the director does not place it yet) |
| `text_emphasis` | a timeline's date as it is said; a comparison's side (its column) when named; a step when named (when `[SYNC]` does not already reveal it); the defined term (if marked up) | the item glows (the page's own narration glow for list items) |
| `text_reveal` | the sentence that says what the code prints (else a share of the scene) | the output card stays hidden until then, then fades in |
| `camera_focus` / `camera_return` | the key teaching moment (the first `[SYNC]` beat, the first step, the look cue), else when the composition planned it (a move of 0.5–4 s); a return to the whole frame at 82 % of a long scene (9 s or more, the focus held at least 4 s) | one camera move from the current framing (framing checked safe by Phase 13) |
| `presenter_point` / `presenter_explain` / `presenter_summarize` | pointing when the attention moves to the visual, the formula or the output (twice at most); explaining at the next sentence; summing up (`presenter_pause` / `presenter_emphasis` are in the vocabulary, not placed yet) | the drawn teacher changes gesture / expression in place (its mouth keeps following the voice); Aadhi changes narration state (it has no pointing); an AI clip cannot act (the visual carries the moment) |

### Rules (educational synchronization)
- Never before its word: an event fires at its position, at most 0.06 s early (the page's lead); its tolerance is the
  lateness budget it is measured against.
- One primary emphasis at a time (two within 0.8 s on different targets: the more important keeps the moment, even
  when it comes second); camera moves never compete for the moment (they are not primary emphasis) and the return
  ranks last; no animation of every sentence (caps per type: 6 labels, 8 text emphases, one camera focus and one
  return, 4 of each other type; at most 24 events).
- The result stays visible: a scene with a result (the code's output, a formula, steps, a timeline) holds it 0.3–0.7 s
  by complexity (0.1 s more for beginners, at most 0.7 s) before the next scene; other scenes advance as before.
- Attention moves presenter → visual → detail → presenter → result (recorded with the plan).
- Orphans out: a visual removed in Visual Review takes its events with it; a board target the page could not find is
  never sent (a feature column, an unmarked term).
- A short scene (under 4 s, or one sentence) is simplified (three moments besides its labels); a scene with no narration
  is placed on the estimate.

### AI-assisted alignment (optional)
In AI-assisted mode only, for the targets the rules could not place by name (an unnamed comparison side, a step
described in other words, the visual without a look cue, the code's output): the model chooses, for each listed target
id, a sentence number — nothing else. Enum ids and in-range numbers only (the first 20 sentences), one bounded repair, at
most 6 scenes per composition within a 25 s budget of its own (Phase 15's `DIRECTOR_AI_BUDGET` setting), a failure or a
timeout leaves the rules in charge; the outcome is kept with the scene. An answer is re-validated when reused; a failed,
late or invalid attempt is shown again, not re-asked, until the scene's sentences or targets change or the user
regenerates that scene (only it is asked again, first, so the cap never skips it); a scene the cap skipped, or one
composed while no model was available, is asked on a later composition.

### Visual Review and the page
- The composition inspector shows a read-only "Synchronization" block: the timing source, up to 8 key moments in plain
  words resolved from the scene ("1.3 s — Force appears as a label · when the narration names it"), the attention flow,
  notes (the presenter cannot act here; moments placed by a share of the scene; the result is held). No timeline editing
  (Phase 19).
- Editing a scene's narration or board in the script editor re-plans the cinematic lesson first, then draws and saves the
  scene once, with its new timing (the direction and composition are recomposed deterministically, so they come back the
  same; no media is generated). A visual decision in Visual Review re-plans too (no orphan moments in the preview).

### Files changed
- **New:** `sync_director.py`, `tests/test_sync_director.py`, `tests/sync.test.js`, `tests/sync_review.test.js`,
  `tests/sync_browser_check.mjs`.
- **Changed:** `composer.py` (the plan's `sync`, presenter capabilities, the AI alignment call), `cinematic.js` (the
  synchronization runtime and its clocks, dispatch, camera moves from the current framing, `syncSummary`; the early-beat
  fix), `presenter.js` (`act`: gesture and expression changed in place), `presenters.py` (`sync_capabilities`,
  `acting_for`, the sync actions and their acting), `review.js` (the Synchronization block, the narration retiming
  helpers, re-plan after a visual decision), `index.html` (server audio for synchronized scenes in preview with a speech fallback, the narration clock
  wiring, presenter acting, the end hold, the script-editor re-plan, styles), `scene_intent.py` (the board's marked words
  in page order, and the definition's own), `visual_director.py` (a huge `[PAUSE:n]` bounded), `tests/test_presenters.py`,
  `tests/presenter.test.js`.

### Results
Silent regression (Chrome with `--disable-audio-output`, speech stubbed, no real AI provider call). Two fixes from the
document's fact-check landed while the full run was under way: the speech-fallback clock (page) and per-scene AI
regeneration (server). They only act in synchronized cinematic scenes. Every suite that had run before them was run
again on the final code, and so were the unit suites:

| Suite | Result |
|---|---|
| Backend (unittest) | **491/491** (439 before + 52 Phase 16) |
| Frontend (node --test) | **180/180** (148 before + 32 Phase 16) |
| Phase 1 mascot | 27/27 |
| Phase 2 export | 30/30 |
| Export with library assets | 35/35 (re-run alone; see below) |
| Phase 3 assets | 26/26 |
| Phase 4 visual router | 24/24 |
| Phase 5 AI cache | 11/11 (re-run alone; see below) |
| Phase 6 Visual Review | 17/17 |
| Phase 7 recording performance (720p) | 18/18 (28.6 fps effective, 0 stalls, page 130.2 fps) |
| Phase 8 providers | 12/12 |
| Phase 9 recovery | 14/14 |
| Phase 10 Manim sandbox | 17/17 |
| Phase 11 source documents | 18/18 |
| Phase 12 presenter | 20/20 |
| Phase 13 cinematic | 24/24 (re-run alone; see below) |
| Phase 14 composer | 17/17 (preview/export frame difference 4.7) |
| Phase 15 direction | 23/23 (preview/export frame difference 5.1) |
| **Phase 16 browser check** | **17/17** (in the full run on the final code; also 17/17 alone just before the last two fixes; preview/export frame difference 4.7) |
| Static checks | inline scripts and all JS files parse; browser checks parse; Python modules compile |

**Phase 13's page frame rate:** in the full run it measured 28 fps (the floor is 30) and the check failed (23/24).
Re-run alone on the final code three times, it passed 24/24 each time (76, 77 and 79 fps). The measurement starts with the slide's
first render. The cause of the slow window in the full run was not identified. Phase 16's per-frame work is one bounded
pass over at most 24 moments, and it stops once they have fired. Nothing in the code was changed for this.

**Two failures in the final-code re-run, each passing when run again alone (no code changed):**
- **Phase 5 AI cache (2/3, the check stopped):** its click on "Generate AI video" timed out. The button was still
  animating and was then redrawn ("element is not stable", then "detached from the DOM"). This is a Classic scene that
  Phase 16 does not touch. The same check passed in the full run and alone (11/11).
- **Export with library assets (34/35):** "frames of every scene … differ from each other" compares JPEG file sizes,
  and two different scenes' frames happened to share a byte count (19,846; their checksums differ and the images
  show different scenes). The export itself was correct. It passed in the full run and alone (35/35).

### LLM validation
**Not done for real.** No text model key exists on this machine. AI-assisted alignment was verified only with the
stand-in model (valid, malformed-then-repaired, fabricated and failing answers) and with a real provider name and no key
(unavailable: the rules decide). No real request was made.

### Visual quality validation (rendered moments)
A representative lesson (introduction, definition, process, diagram, the diagram continued, formula, code, comparison,
timeline, quiz, summary; the lesson's own library diagrams; the Aadhi Teacher) was planned automatically and played in
real Chrome at 1280×720 with the server's voice (silent: audio output disabled). A frame was captured at every
synchronization moment and inspected over two rounds; the first round was not accepted (see Issues):
- **Definition:** the camera leans in, and "Photosynthesis" is outlined on the board as the narration says it. The
  teacher points at the board.
- **Diagram:** the leaf diagram pulses when the narration points at it. Each part on the board glows as it is named
  ("The palisade cells catch the light", "The stoma lets air in"). The teacher points, then explains with open hands.
- **Formula:** F = ma pulses at its `[SYNC]` reveal and the camera leans in. The symbol labels slide in as each meaning
  is spoken (F = Force, m = Mass, a = Acceleration).
- **Code:** the output card stays hidden while the code is explained, then fades in on "It prints the numbers zero to
  four".
- **Comparison:** "Let us compare plants and animals" lights the Plants column, then the Animals column (the first one
  stops glowing). The teacher points.
- **Timeline:** each event appears and glows as its date is said (1837, 1946, 1971, 2007); the camera returns for the
  whole line.
- **Summary:** the teacher sums up with an open hand while the four key points stay on screen.
- **The quiz** runs its own flow (no synchronization plan); the introduction and the process have one moment each.

Measured: 25 moments with a measurable word position, maximum error 0.05 s and mean 0.04 s (0.06 s and 0.04 s in the
full regression), never more than the page's 0.06 s lead before the word. At speech rate 1.3 the same moments still land on their words. Every scene ends with all its
moments fired and no result hidden; the scenes with a result hold it before the next scene (0.5 s measured on the code
scene). Nothing flickers: at most one board emphasis at a time, and the camera never competes with a result.

**Preview versus export:** the export recorded 28 moments, all on time, at the same planned positions as the preview.
Its frame of the code scene matches the preview's: mean difference 4.7 of 255 on a 32×18 grid, and no player controls in
the export. Layout at 1920×1080 is unchanged by synchronization (it only times what the composition placed). The
composer and direction checks verify both sizes, and their scenes now carry synchronization plans. No AI
generation ran (the library diagrams were reused).

### Issues found and fixed during the phase
- **Found by integrating with the existing tests (none changed for these):**
  - Phase 13's rule that a plan points to the lesson's text and never copies it: the first synchronization plans carried
    the concept's words. Concepts are references now (`{kind, index}`: a label, a step, a column…), resolved on the page.
  - Label texts were read from the stored scene instead of the direction being composed (the label moments could follow
    a stale direction); the presenter's acting carried empty values or a narration state the validator refused
    (the whole plan was dropped); a camera moment lasting the rest of a long scene broke Phase 13's 10 s bound (capped
    at 4 s, the return follows).
- **Found by the director's tests (each pinned by a test first):** two moments in conflict both stayed when the second
  was the more important; a sentence ending with a number ("… in 1971.") was not a sentence end; a narration with
  thousands of `[PAUSE]` segments was not bounded (200 segments, a pause at most 60 s); a comparison with a leading
  feature column focused the wrong column; a definition's term was emphasised when the board did not mark it up (the page
  had nothing to light); a timeline's events were counted in a different order from the page's items.
- **Found by measuring in the browser:** moments fired up to 0.23 s before their word (the page subtracted the whole
  tolerance from the due time). A moment now fires at its position, 0.06 s early at most; its tolerance is the lateness
  budget it is measured against. First run: errors up to 0.39 s (mean 0.25 s); on the final code: at most 0.06 s (mean 0.04 s).
- **Found by the end-of-phase security audit and correctness review (two read-only agents; each verified, fixed and
  covered by a test):**
  - A crafted narration (a long run of punctuation) made the sentence splitter quadratic; the inspector's board reader
    could backtrack on an unclosed tag or comment. Both patterns are linear now.
  - A kept AI alignment record sent back by the page could crash the composition, and a deeply nested one raised a
    recursion error: both are caught (the rules decide). A failed alignment was asked again on every composition: it is
    shown and reused until the scene changes or the user regenerates.
  - Phase 15's narration estimate trusted `[PAUSE:n]` (a huge n overflowed the estimate): bounded at 60 s.
  - A short scene lost its labels and its camera move in the simplification: labels are always kept, and a scene
    whose plan has no camera moment (a short scene, a zoom-out) plays its planned camera move as before.
  - A comparison's second side was dropped when the table had a feature column; the director counted terms without
    duplicates while the page counts every marked word (the wrong word could light).
  - Editing a scene's narration in the script editor drew the scene twice and saved the version before the new timing
    to the server; a board-only edit did not retime. The lesson is re-planned first, then drawn and saved once; a board
    change counts too.
  - A synchronized scene whose server voice failed in the preview stayed silent (no browser-voice fallback): the failed
    segment is now spoken by the browser on the speech clock.
  - A presenter moment could replace Aadhi's question or success state: Aadhi changes state only while talking.
  - A visual removed in Visual Review kept its moments until the next re-plan: a visual decision re-plans the lesson.
- **Found by looking at the rendered moments (see Visual quality validation):**
  - A timeline's date is spoken before the `[SYNC]` that reveals its item, so the date's glow landed on an item still
    hidden (the moment was lost): the item the narration names is shown with its glow.
  - A comparison's first side kept glowing when the second was named (the whole table lit): one emphasis holds the board
    at a time.
- **Found by a read-only fact-check of this document against the code (each fixed and covered by a test):**
  - The browser-voice fallback restarted the speech clock at the first segment, so when the server voice failed at a
    later segment its moments never came due: the clock now starts at the segment that failed (and the fallback uses
    the selected voice, shows its items and subtitle as the browser-voice preview does).
  - Regenerating one scene's alignment re-asked every scene's failed answer (and the cap could skip the scene the user
    regenerated), while a scene the cap had skipped, or composed while no model was available, was never asked again:
    a regeneration asks only its scene, first; skipped and unavailable scenes are asked on a later composition.
  - Several statements of this document were corrected (the end hold applies to scenes with a result; `label_exit`,
    presenter pause and emphasis are vocabulary the director does not place yet; the diagram focus cap includes the look
    cue; the tolerances are lateness budgets checked by the browser test, not enforced by the page).
- **Test expectations changed with the behaviour** (none weakened): four renderer tests expected a moment up to its
  tolerance early (now: at its word); the retiming test that compared narrations only (now: the board counts too).

### Known limitations
- **No word timing exists.** A moment's position is a character ratio inside its narration segment, the same rule the
  page already used for `[SYNC]`. It is exact for the test server's tone and approximate for a real voice, which is not
  uniform. In a long segment a moment can land a fraction of a second away from its word; the browser check holds
  each moment to its lateness budget, but only against the test server's tone. Edge-TTS word boundaries would make it exact, but the existing audio path discards them, and changing that
  path was out of scope.
- **Concepts are found by their words.** The director looks for a label's meaning, a term, a column header, a date or a
  step's words in the narration. A paraphrase is not found by the rules. AI-assisted alignment can place it in AI mode;
  otherwise the moment falls back to a share of the scene or is left out.
- **Emphasis targets are what the page can find:**
  - the visual as a whole (no diagram regions or nodes, since diagrams carry no structure);
  - top-level list items, table columns, the two sides of a two-list comparison;
  - the marked terms, the code's output card, the formula.
  Highlighting a single code line or a diagram part needs structured content.
- **Presenter acting is in place, not animated:**
  - The drawn teacher switches gesture and expression at a moment (no tween between poses); its mouth keeps following
    the voice.
  - Aadhi changes narration state only.
  - An AI video presenter cannot act, so the visual carries those moments.
- **Browser speech (a preview fallback only):** only each segment's start is known, so the moments inside a segment
  follow the estimate.
- **The synchronization is read-only in Visual Review.** Moving or removing a moment by hand belongs to Phase 19's
  editor.
- **AI-assisted alignment** was verified with the stand-in model only (no text model key on this machine).

### Deferred to later phases
Phase 17 (styling system), Phase 18 (quality engine), Phase 19 (editor: no timeline editing here), Phase 20, Phase 21.
The narration model, the event vocabulary and the synchronization plan are the extension points.

### Final status
**Complete.**
- **Verified end to end:** every narrated content scene of a representative lesson gets one synchronization plan. Its
  moments fire on the narration's audio clock at their words, played silently in real Chrome with the server's voice:
  - definition term; diagram look cue and its parts; formula reveal and symbol labels;
  - code output reveal; comparison sides; timeline dates; presenter point, explain and sum up;
  - camera focus and return; the end hold.
  The same moments land on time at speech rate 1.3. New narration recompiles the timing and nothing is generated. A
  removed visual takes its moments with it. Visual Review shows the synchronization in plain words, a refresh rebuilds
  the same plans, and the export records the same moments on time as the preview.
- **Not verified:** AI-assisted alignment with a real Gemini or OpenAI model. There is no key here; the stand-in model
  and the unavailable path were tested. A real voice's word timing is also unverified: positions are character ratios,
  measured exact against the test server's tone.
- **Unchanged:**
  - The Classic style (synchronization exists in the cinematic style only).
  - Automatic mode makes no model call.
  - The narration and TTS pipeline: no second TTS, subtitle or animation engine.
  - Phases 12–15's responsibilities.
  - No styling-system work (Phase 17).
- **Nothing committed or deployed.** No database change: the synchronization lives in the scene's cinematic plan.

---

## Phase 17 — Professional Video Styling System

### Objective
Give every generated lesson a deliberate visual identity: a style family (Academic, Cinematic Education, Children's
Education, Corporate Training) expressed as structured, versioned tokens — colour roles, typography, spacing, shapes,
surfaces, background treatment, motion, transition and camera preferences, presenter / visual / caption frames — that
the existing cinematic page consumes. Style decides how a scene looks. What it teaches (Phase 15), its composition
(Phase 14), when things happen (Phase 16), the rendering (Phase 13), the presenter's identity (Phase 12) and the media
(the Visual Router, the Asset Library, the AI media layer) are unchanged. Phase 18 (quality engine) was not started.

### Baseline (start of Phase 17)
Backend 491/491, frontend 180/180, Phase 1 27/27, Phase 2 30/30, export with library assets 35/35, Phase 3 26/26,
Phase 4 24/24, Phase 5 11/11, Phase 6 17/17, Phase 7 18/18, Phase 8 12/12, Phase 9 14/14, Phase 10 17/17,
Phase 11 18/18, Phase 12 20/20, Phase 13 24/24, Phase 14 17/17, Phase 15 23/23, Phase 16 17/17.

### Audit (before Phase 17; two read-only analysts plus the orchestrator)
- **Style today:** a lesson "typography" (`academic` | `modern`) choosing a background palette and the title font; motion,
  transitions, background and emphasis settings (emphasis had no visible effect); kept per browser (localStorage), not
  with the lesson; every plan already carried `plan.style`.
- **The look:** about 600 hard-coded colours in the page (gold #FFD700 and purples), ~75 CSS variables in all; the
  cinematic CSS block is scoped to `body[data-cinematic]` (Classic never gets it), the board's content CSS is shared with
  Classic.
- **Fingerprints:** the lesson's style keys are part of the composition fingerprint (a change re-opens approvals); the
  rendered plan hash excludes the style; AI backgrounds put the typography's words in the prompt (the cache identity).
- **Export:** records the same tab (styling matches by construction); 16:9 only (no 9:16 or 1:1 path exists).
- **Persistence:** `/save-history` writes a new row per save from fixed fields; no database change is needed (the lesson
  JSON carries new keys).

### Architecture
```
 lesson settings: style family (+ version) + a few bounded overrides      scene: its own accent / background (Visual Review)
   │  (saved with the lesson: payload.cinematic_style)                        │
   └──────────────► styles.py  (the style registry: 4 families × versions, semantic tokens, preferences,
                    resolve → accessibility guard → effective style + fingerprint)
                        │  once per scene, on the server, when the composition is planned
                        ▼
   scene.cinematic_plan.style.look  { id "academic@1", tone, prefs, overrides, scene_overrides, adjustments,
                                      fingerprint, legacy, css { --st-<token>: value … ~110 } }
                        │  the same plan for the preview and the export
                        ▼
   the Phase 13 page (cinematic.js CinematicStage.layout → applyLook): CSS custom properties on <html>,
   body[data-cine-style / -tone / -emphasis / -caption / -pframe / -vframe / -motion-level]
                        ▼
   index.html CSS: the cinematic rules for colour, type and shape read var(--st-<token>, <today's value>)  ·  charts read
   the chart tokens
```
- **One resolver, on the server.** The page never works out a style: it applies the plan's values (re-checked: names
  `--st-[a-z0-9-]`, values through the same allow-list as the server — only the listed CSS functions, no `url` or
  `expression`, quotes only around font names — at most 300). Preview and export are the same page reading the same plan,
  so they cannot differ (apart from a viewer's reduced-motion setting, which only the preview follows).
- **Nothing per frame.** A style is resolved once per scene (well under 1.5 ms on the server; 200 scenes in 46–84 ms) and
  applied once per scene
  (only the variables that changed are written); entrance timing is read once per scene.
- **Responsibilities unchanged.** Phase 15 decides what teaches, Phase 14 the composition, Phase 16 when things happen,
  Phase 13 renders and owns the camera, Phase 12 the presenter's identity, the router / asset library / AI media layer the
  media. A style never changes the lesson's motion, transition or background setting, a camera move, a synchronized
  moment, a presenter or a visual.

### The style contract (`styles.py`)
- **Tokens** (the CSS variable `--st-<name>`), grouped: background (`bg-1..3`, `bg-solid`, glows, vignette, pattern,
  scrim), surfaces (`surface-1/2`, border, shadow, panels, definition / formula fills), text (`text`, `text-secondary`,
  `text-muted`, `heading`, `title-text`, shadows), colour roles (`accent`, `accent-text`, `on-accent`, `secondary`,
  `focus-rgb`, info / success / error, callouts), tables, code and output panels, captions (text, box, highlight, size),
  labels, title bar, presenter frame (border, radius, shadow, drop shadow), visual frame, chart colours, typography
  (title / heading / body / code / caption fonts, weights, case, tracking, text scale, line height, label size), spacing
  (`space-xs..xl`, board padding), shapes (`radius-sm/md/lg/chip`), motion (easing, durations, entrance length and
  distance).
- **Preferences** (enums): tone (dark / light), emphasis (glow / outline / soft — always a shape plus colour, never colour
  alone), captions (shadow / box), presenter frame (none / clean / card / rounded) and name card, visual frame
  (panel / card / rounded / plain), background pattern (none / dots / grid / paper), motion (low / standard), camera
  (still / subtle) and transition preferences.
- **Fonts:** only what the page already loads (Inter, Outfit, JetBrains Mono) and the system serif (Georgia): nothing is
  downloaded.
- **Composition, not inheritance:** each family is the base plus its own values (no deep hierarchy); versions are kept
  side by side (`academic@1`); a lesson keeps the version it was made with (`style_version`), an unknown one resolves to
  the latest the server knows.
- **Fingerprint:** the schema, family, version, legacy variant, the lesson's overrides and the scene's (nothing else).

### The four families
| Family | Look | Typography | Surfaces and frames | Motion / transition / camera preferences |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Academic** | light: warm ivory paper, ink-navy text, crimson accent, faint ruled texture | serif titles and headings (Georgia), Inter body, no capitals | white cards, thin rules, small radii, captions in a dark box, emphasis as an outline | low (plain fades), fade, subtle camera |
| **Cinematic Education** (default) | dark: deep indigo and violet with soft light, gold accent — the page's existing look | Outfit titles, capital headings, Inter light body | translucent violet card, gold edge, glow emphasis | standard entrances, fade, subtle camera |
| **Children's Education** | light: warm cream and sky blue, orange accent, gentle dots | Outfit throughout, slightly larger text (×1.06), more spacing | rounded white cards, large radii, rounded presenter frame with a name card, soft emphasis, captions in a box | standard, soft fade, subtle camera |
| **Corporate Training** | dark: crisp navy, sky-blue accent, faint grid | Inter throughout, no capitals, tight tracking | flat navy cards, small radii, a presenter card with a name card, outline emphasis | low (plain fades), crossfade, subtle camera |

Code panels stay dark in every style (the page's Prism theme is dark); the code's output card follows the style.

### The user's choices (bounded) and precedence
- **Lesson:** the style, then accent colour (gold, coral, crimson, teal, sky, violet, emerald — each with a variant for dark
  and light styles), text size (standard / large / larger), animation (low / standard), background (plain / subtle / rich),
  caption size; advanced: code size, formula size, diagram frame. Nothing else is exposed; no raw colour, size, font,
  CSS or URL is accepted anywhere (the API answers 422 for unknown keys or values; a save drops them). The choices belong
  to a chosen style: a lesson that keeps
  its original look shows no style selected, the choices wait until one is picked, and the server ignores them without a
  style (they could not be saved with it).
- **Scene:** only accent and background, through Visual Review's composition Change form (a scene may stand out, the
  lesson stays consistent).
- **Precedence:** accessibility (contrast, readable sizes) > the user's explicit choices (lesson settings, Visual Review) >
  the decisions of Phases 16 / 15 / 14 (timing, teaching intent, composition) > the style's preferences > decorative
  defaults. Picking a style changes only the style: its transition and camera preferences are offered as a suggestion
  ("This style suggests Soft fade transitions." — **Use the style's suggestions**), applied only when the
  user clicks it. (An automatic pre-fill was tried first: it re-opened every Visual Review approval and could turn a
  deliberate still camera into a moving one.)

### Accessibility
- The main text colours are checked against what they sit on (WCAG relative luminance, translucent surfaces composited
  over the background): body text, secondary text, highlighted words, highlighted words in code (on the code panel), table
  headers, dates (on the card and on their badge tint), callouts, labels, the code's output, text on the accent and
  captions ≥ 4.5:1; titles and headings ≥ 3:1; the accent itself where
  it draws bullets, ticks and step numbers ≥ 3:1 (non-text). A colour that fails is moved toward whichever end (black or
  white) can reach the ratio, and the adjustment is reported in plain words (Visual Review). The built-in families and
  accents need no adjustment.
- Over a picture or clip background, the non-default styles put the scene title and label chips on the style's own
  surface (a light style's dark text never lands on a dark picture).
- On light styles, a board colour written inline in the lesson's HTML that would not read on the light card is replaced by
  the style's text colour (the page's contrast guard; restored when the style changes).
- Text sizes are bounded (0.9–1.3×); the page's fit still shrinks a board that would overflow (never below 80 %).
- Captions stay in the subtitle band; on light styles they sit in a dark box.
- Reduced motion: the preview's calm path (plain fades, still camera, moving transitions become fades) and, in CSS, the
  decorative loops (breathing, blinking, shimmer) stop.

### Persistence, versions and invalidation
- The lesson's style is saved with the lesson (`cinematic_style`, cleaned: an unknown family or override is never stored)
  by every save, and a style change while the lesson is open is stored in place (`POST /api/cinematic/style`, no new
  history entry; a change made while a save is on its way follows the new lesson). Opening a lesson restores its style; a
  lesson saved before Phase 17 has none and keeps its original look. No database change.
- **What a style change invalidates:** only the style-derived rendering. The composition fingerprint (Visual Review
  approvals) is unchanged — the style's colours and type are not part of it, and the lesson's motion / transition /
  background keys stay as they were (Phase 13's rule for them is unchanged). Visual approvals, direction, synchronization
  timing, presenter plans, assets and AI media are untouched; nothing is regenerated. A scene's own accent / background
  (Visual Review) is not its layout either: changing or undoing only those keeps an approved layout approved.
- **AI media:** scene visuals' requests never include the style (media identity and presentation style stay separate).
  An AI background (generated only on request) uses the style family's words; Cinematic Education uses the default
  typography's words, the ones the backgrounds were generated with before, so the AI cache reuses them (the same identity),
  and a legacy lesson keeps its typography's words.
- **Old lessons:** no style → `cinematic_education@1` with `legacy: true`, whose tokens are the page's existing values
  (the plan hash and composition fingerprint are identical to before Phase 17).

### Presenter, visuals, charts
- **Presenter frame** (Phase 12's presenter is never changed): the drawn teacher's drop shadow, an AI clip's or image's
  border, radius and shadow, and the frame kind (none / clean / card / rounded) follow the style. Children's Education and
  Corporate Training add a name card with the presenter profile's own name (text only), inside the presenter's layer so it
  rides with the camera and disappears with the layer (the entrance animates only the presenter); never for Aadhi (his
  studio clip is full-frame) and never while the presenter is hidden or its clip is missing.
- **Visual frame:** the side picture and chart panels take the style's frame (panel, card, rounded, plain); a library asset
  is framed, never altered; the other side panels (graph, 3D, GIF, clip) keep their own dark look.
- **Charts** read the current scene's chart colours from its plan (the chart is drawn before the scene's look is applied);
  a lesson with no style chosen keeps today's colours; pie, doughnut and polar charts and per-bar colours keep the lesson's
  own colours (one colour per slice would make them unreadable). The data never changes.
- **Code** panels stay dark in every style (Prism's dark theme); **formulas** (MathJax) take the text colour, the formula
  panel and the formula size choice.

### Files changed
- **New:** `styles.py`, `tests/test_styles.py`, `tests/style.test.js`, `tests/style_review.test.js`, `tests/style_wiring.test.js`,
  `tests/style_browser_check.mjs`.
- **Changed:** `cinematic.py` (the effective style in each plan, the style family's palette, settings and scene override
  validation, the vocabulary's `looks`, `POST /api/cinematic/style`, the background route's style words, new transitions,
  the review route: a style-only change keeps an approval, malformed records ignored), `server.py` (`cinematic_style` in
  `/save-history`), `cinematic.js` (applying a look per scene, entrance timing and emphasis colour from tokens, new
  transitions, the light-style contrast guard, the presenter name card, the settings panel's style picker, suggestions and
  choices, `styleSummary`), `review.js` (the Style block, the scene accent / background selects), `index.html` (the
  tokenized cinematic CSS, the board content / captions / presenter / visual frames per style, reduced motion, the picker
  and review styles; saving, restoring and storing the lesson's style; the review adapter; chart colours).

### Results

final code (the two small fixes made after the full run's backend step were followed by a second backend and frontend
run):

| Frontend (node --test) | **211/211** (180 before + 31 Phase 17) |
| Phase 1 mascot | 27/27 |

| Export with library assets | 35/35 |
